ParlaMint-DK_20191212103804	First of all, I would like to thank the authors of this motion for a resolution. This is a very important agenda, and I believe that everyone shares the concern about deforestation in Brazil and also in other parts of the world, because forest is of course crucial to the environment, biodiversity and climate. The European Commission has described the threats to the world's forests as one of the greatest sustainability challenges of our time. Unfortunately, soy production and cattle farming are often associated with deforestation. In the context of soya production, there are also reports of social problems, problems with working conditions and working conditions, and quite extensive use of pesticides. We must not simply accept that the goods that we import into Denmark and the European Union are responsible for deforestation in the world, either directly or indirectly. But how should we act on the Danish side to combat deforestation? The government is working on several fronts to promote sustainable and deforestation-free agricultural production. We must not least be on the agenda in Europe through active participation in the so-called Amsterdam partnership, where we work together with the United Kingdom, France, Italy, the Netherlands, Germany and Norway to promote the import of sustainable and deforested agricultural raw materials. This is among other things. Following pressure and calls from the Amsterdam partnership, the European Commission presented a communication on deforestation this summer. On the Danish side, we are pressing for ambitious EU action to follow up the communication, and the government is strongly committed to the need for significant efforts to combat deforestation. It is therefore also very positive that this area is now included in the Commission communication on a green deal which was launched yesterday. This shows, of course, that the Commission will come forward with measures, including regulatory measures, to promote deforestation-free value chains. I expect us to agree on ambitious Council conclusions on the communication on deforestation at the Agriculture Council as early as Monday, thereby sending a strong signal to the Commission to give high priority to these efforts. But of course it is not enough for us in Denmark to be active at European level. There is also a lot to do at home, and I am therefore very pleased that in September the Danish Alliance for Responsible Soja was launched, consisting of trade organisations, food companies, supermarket chains and NGOs, and which my own ministry has also joined. The Alliance's vision is that all soya imported into Denmark is produced responsibly, including produced legally and does not contribute to deforestation or conversion of other natural vegetation. The Danish Alliance for Responsible Soja will contribute to the demand for responsiblely produced and deforested soya only in Denmark. Some companies in Denmark have already taken responsibility and have taken the lead, and they should inspire the rest of the industry. The Ministry of the Environment and Food fully supports the alliance and, as mentioned, is actively involved in the work. The Ministry would also very much like to contribute to greater clarity and bring more facts on the table on relevant issues for the soya value chains. This will benefit all of us in promoting sustainable and deforestation-free agricultural production. In Denmark we import approx. 1,6-1,7 million This means that, in the international context, we are a small player. But that is by no means an argument for not taking responsibility. Firstly, we have an objective that Danish farmers in the future significantly reduce imports of feed protein, especially soya, and instead replace it with Danish-produced feed protein, for example. multi-annual grasses. Secondly, we need to work together in the European Union to promote more sustainable soya production, for the European Union is, in relative terms, a major player who imports more than 30 million people overall. t soy per year, e.g. from South America. Basically, the government's position is that Denmark's international commitment must be based on dialogue and cooperation, because it is the best way to promote European values and standards also in relation to the countries with which we disagree and therefore an import ban is not the way forward if we are to influence developments in Brazil. Instead, we must put pressure on the Brazilian Government in other ways and engage in constructive dialogue to help ensure that Brazil meets its climate and environmental responsibilities. We can do that, for example. through the EU's trade agreements, and therefore it is also positive that the draft EU agreement with Mercosur, of which Brazil is a part, contains an ambitious chapter on trade and sustainable development. The Parties undertake to contribute to sustainable development and also build on their multilateral commitments in the field of the environment. As an example of the strong sustainability clauses in the agreement, a commitment to the implementation of the Paris Agreement is made, in which Brazil has accepted ambitious commitments to reduce deforestation and reforestation. The European Union will be closely monitoring the compliance of Mercosur countries with their obligations under the agreement and, if the EU considers that Brazil does not fulfil its obligations, the Mercosur Agreement contains a procedure for binding dispute resolution. In this way, the Mercosur agreement actually gives teeth to the Paris Agreement and is therefore a very important lever for the European Union, among other things. In relation to deforestation. I would also like to mention that the Mercosur Agreement also contains an obligation not to promote trade at the expense of social rights or workers' rights. Both parties undertake to respect the ILO rules on forced labour and child labour, the prohibition of discrimination at work, freedom of association and the right to collective bargaining. The complete abandonment of the Mercosur agreement is therefore short-sighted and, moreover, will not change President Bolsonaro's actions. In relation to the specific proposal in the motion for a resolution on a stop to imports, we in Denmark will not be able to make an independent decision on this, because trade policy, as we know, is EU competence, and an import ban, if necessary, would also have to comply with WTO rules. And there it will be immensely difficult to imagine how it could be done if it were only to apply to Brazil and at the same time to be WTO-friendly, because here the principle is, as a starting point, equal treatment for all countries. As I have explained, the government's position is that an import ban is not an appropriate way to go, because, as I have said, there are many other good, important ways to go. Against this background, the government rejects the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212112224	Thank you. There are no brief comments. We would like to thank the rapporteur and move on to the next rapporteur, Mr President. Orla Østerby, Conservative People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212112244	Thank you. First of all, the Conservative People's Party cannot, of course, support motion for a resolution B 15. I would also like to say that the government would not be able to impose an import ban on beef or soya from Brazil, even if they disagree with the political line and statements of the President of Brazil, and even if they are concerned about the burning of forests in Brazil. Denmark and the EU have concluded a trade agreement within the World Trade Organisation (WTO) with, among other things: Brazil, which means that we cannot ban imports of beef or soya, for example, unless the products themselves are dangerous. We are usually pleased about this, because it also means that the countries to which we export cannot simply shut down their imports of Danish goods. The Danish economy and employment are entirely dependent on a well-functioning international trading system. This also means that we can export our goods without leg buckles. We are also pleased that the trade agreements prevent large and strong Western economies, such as the United States, from being able to do so. The European Union or the United States can suddenly choose to close down exports from developing countries, for example. to push them to conform to a policy that the countries importing their response want them to follow. The trade agreements represent a very great advantage for the weakest countries and the smallest economies. Even if the government could, it would not make sense to impose a Danish import ban on soya and beef from Brazil. In particular, it does not make sense with regard to soya. We are taking away an infinitely small part of the soy produced by Brazil, much less than 1%, so that it would not have any effect on conditions in Brazil. The Brazilians would simply be able to sell the soy elsewhere. Probably a Danish ban would be a welcome contribution to the Brazilian President's story of a neo-imperialist Western Europe, which continues to believe that it can dictate to the subjects of the former colonies how they should behave. If we ban the import of soya for livestock production, we will move it to other countries where there is no ban, and they will then import soya instead of us. Or Brazilians can feed their own chickens with soy and then export the chickens to Denmark. This will result in fewer jobs in Denmark, but will not protect the rain forests. What can you actually do? We can influence Brazilians through the EU and the Mercosur agreement. The EU has just negotiated a trade agreement with Mercosur countries, including Brazil. This agreement may be the best way to discuss environmental issues with Brazilians. The agreement contains an obligation for the parties to the agreement not to roll back their environmental legislation and to ensure implementation and compliance with their own environmental legislation. But it does not actually seem to be happening in Brazil right now, so the obvious question to the government and to the European Commission is what we will do on that occasion. However, we must be careful that the discussion is not used to promote a protectionist agenda. We must influence the Brazilians through our trade cooperation with them. We can help influence Brazilians and other importers when we deal with them. This is not a task that we as a small country can undertake ourselves, but we can do so together with other European importers. The feed industry at home has long been working to ensure that all feed importers in the European Union have to make demands on their suppliers that soya be responsibly produced. That work could be significantly strengthened if the governments of the EU agreed to put pressure on all European countries' importers so that they demand that the soya coming to Europe be produced responsibly. The government should work to include in an action plan to be drawn up by the Commission in order to intensify the EU's efforts to protect and restore the world's forests. Similarly, pressure should be put on European and Danish importers of Brazilian beef and of products such as: chicken meat, where soya has been used in production, to ensure that the products have been produced responsibly. We must make demands ourselves, even though our imports from Brazil are minimal. And although we cannot influence the Brazilians, we should nevertheless demand that the soya imported be produced responsibly.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212115743	The debate is open. Sir. Orla Sea, Social Democracy.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212115803	Thank you. L 81 is based on a broad settlement reached under the previous government. It would not be wrong to claim that L 81 is a discretionary mix of opportunities for initiative within the provisions of the Planning Act. The draft law translates the parts of the agreement which deal with shortened time limits by laying out cottage areas in the coastal area by introducing a so-called sunset clause from 8 years to 4 years and requiring an additional statement in planning for Green Denmark Card. In addition, the draft law aims to increase the possibility for municipalities to plan for housing on areas of smell. The proposal also provides for an obligation on the municipalities to explain the extent to which planning for the development of natural areas within Green Denmark Cards is to be implemented. In addition, the flexible housing scheme is transferred from the Housing Regulation Act to the Planning Act. Finally, the draft law amends the Law on the Sea Plan, which lays down the framework for planning the Danish marine waters. It is a wonderful mix, and I certainly should not say that all these initiatives have grown in the Socialist Gardens. We acknowledge that we reached a settlement with the previous government, and the scenario has probably been the classic one: that the government had some desires for less regulation and less planning, and we had some desire to be involved, so that after all things were done under orderly conditions, and so that some green footprints were secured, and that citizens could recognise and see where they stand in relation to their interests. In the name of good government and cooperation, we have gone a long way in meeting each other... the government and the conciliation parties between us... and we agree with that, and we stand by the agreements we have made. It is therefore a good old-fashioned compromise in which most people can see their own contributions translated, but also have to accept that they did not quite stay as they might wish, but they must also accept the contributions of others to the bill. We therefore support the draft law.

This is all quite reasonable. What has actually attracted the greatest attention has been the possibility that the municipalities can plan housing on areas suffering from smell. This requires, as the draft law says, that the Minister for Business and Industry should be involved, and it is, of course, a little special that we are faced with a situation in which we openly allow housing to be built where the limits of smell have been exceeded. But it is a wish that has been, and we have had to strike a balance in it. Basically, we want municipalities to have more local decision-making power, which is why they must have this opportunity, but on the other hand it is also important that we do not contribute to a conflict that we see in different places, between housing and occupations • especially when it is professions that have been there first. I think we have all heard stories from business people who feel pressured by the fact that housing has come close, and it has been a balance to find a compromise. We have had legislative proposals on this in the past, which we could not finish because we simply could not land it properly, in other words where the requirement to build hermetically sealed dwellings was assessed too restrictively by some of the municipalities, but it was to provide security for the companies. A new model has now been found which, in our view, in the Left still ensures the necessary balance, namely that housing development can be made in ports where it is desirable from a municipal point of view, but at the same time with respect for the companies already located in the area. That is why the demands and information are being put in order so that as a resident you know what you are going to do, for example. that there is a company that smells a little more than was hoped for, but it is therefore with open eyes that you move into such a property, and it should not be held to the disadvantage of the company. I hope we've landed it right. At any rate, this is something we have spent a lot of time on, and there have been a lot of people and smart people around, and I hope that we have got it right. At any rate, this is something that we on the Left want to keep an eye on, so we are sure that we have it right. We believe that we have done so, but we must, of course, keep a close eye on it. As I said, this was the result of a political agreement, so we naturally support the bill from the Left.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212123243	The debate is open, and it is first the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212123302	First of all, I would like to make it clear that, in my view, the resolution focuses on a very important issue. For it is clear that the legislation adopted in Brussels and the judgments handed down by the European Court of Justice affect Danish legislation on foreigners. I am therefore a little sorry if some in this Parliament find that the Folketing is kept in ignorance of the decisions Denmark is taking in the European Union, or about the legal proceedings before the European Court of Justice which may have an impact on the territory of foreigners. It is probably especially because the government, including the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration, is doing a great deal to keep the Danish Parliament informed, and it must of course be so. We can mean a little differently. One can of course mean what one wants, about the value and challenges of our EU membership, and the resolution will also be seen as an expression that there may be some nuances in our positions on this in the Folketing, and that is, of course, quite fair. But, of course, it must also be the case that one can form one's opinions on an enlightened basis and, as is also apparent from the parliamentary response to which the proposers refer in the proposal. This is therefore the answer to question 24 of the European Parliamentary Committee. In Denmark, we have a number of procedures to ensure parliamentary involvement and transparency in the work on EU matters. Of course, I am thinking first of all of the special Danish EU decision-making procedure. It is a procedure that ensures that the Folketing is involved as early as possible, while at the same time creating the best basis for defending Danish interests in the European Union. Following this procedure, the Committee on European Affairs will be presented with all important EU cases for information and all matters of greater scope for negotiation. In addition, the committee plays a central role as a mandatesr when we define the Danish position on EU cases. The European Committee is also informed both in writing and orally of significant legal proceedings before the European Court of Justice, in which the government has decided to enter. At least I myself have been a part of that in my time as Minister. As the proposers themselves also mention, in cases where the European Court of Justice gives a ruling which means that we in Denmark have to adjust our legislation or our administrative practice, a note on this is sent to the Folketing, and those notes are publicly available to everyone. There are therefore procedures in place to ensure transparency on EU matters through the involvement and regular briefing of the Folketing on all important issues in the EU area. In addition, our own legislative procedure is designed to ensure that, when a legislative proposal is submitted, the ministries always explain any EU legal aspects of the proposal in question. All laws and regulations implementing legislation from the EU are also provided with a note stating the EU legal background for the new rules. It is therefore clearly declared for both the Folketing and the public when a new Danish legislation is based on or influenced by legislation adopted in the EU. Finally, I would like to stress that, of course, my door is always open and that I always want to run if there is a need for further clarification of issues in specific EU cases in the field of foreigners. In the motion for a resolution, the proposers also state that the government does not have an overview of the influence of EU law on Danish immigration policy and that the desired explanation is necessary in order for the government to be able to act firmly and properly in the field of foreigners. As far as I am concerned, I would like to admit that EU law is a rather large and sometimes incalculable size, and there is also sometimes a ruling by the European Court of Justice which means that we have to adjust our legislation or administrative practices, even if we would have preferred to do so, and that is of course always a pity. Frankly, however, I have little difficulty in seeing how a long list of previous judgments and acts could prevent or prevent the European Court of Justice from giving a new ruling in the future, which Denmark disagrees with. Instead, it is crucial that we continue and proactively assert our influence. It is the government's view that in many areas strong EU cooperation, for example, makes very good sense. in relation to the common IT systems in the field of aliens, such as: supports the fight against cross-border crime. There is also a great deal to be gained from the fact that Danish citizens and Danish companies can benefit from free movement within the Union. Sometimes, then, there are decisions taken in the European Union, or judgments handed down, with which we are less in agreement, and I myself think I have been completely open about that, and the government is generally quite open about that. In general, however, we in the government, like all previous governments before us and a broad majority in the Parliament, find that all in all, it is best to be part of the European Union. Denmark must remain an active participant in EU cooperation and seek maximum influence. We must continue to monitor developments closely and take care of our interests in the best possible way, for example. by going to the European Court of Justice and giving our opinion when new material cases are dealt with by the European Court of Justice. So it is not because the matter is not important that the government cannot support the proposal; the government just does not agree that here is the right method, the right way to use resources. Instead of paying a professor to make a longer historical analysis, we must constantly be in the work clothes and try to influence EU cooperation in a direction that best serves Denmark's interests. With those words, I would like to thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212132842	Thank you for the floor. In New Citizens we stand guard over the freedoms of the Danes and the Danish people's government. But all experience shows that migrants from African and Arab countries often cannot and will not integrate into Danish society. On the contrary, the many migrants create huge cultural and economic problems for Denmark. Therefore, Denmark must decide for itself which foreigners can obtain residence in Denmark and who can obtain citizenship. Let me mention two new cases, both of which create confusion as to what scope the EU considers Denmark should be left to pursue an independent foreign policy, and this is certainly the case, even though the judgments are far-reaching. Firstly, there is the judgment C-227/17, delivered on 12 March 2019, where the European Court of Justice is now demanding that Denmark place emphasis on its links with the whole of Europe and not just with Denmark. The European Union therefore believes that it should not be a link with Denmark, but with the whole of the European Union, which decides whether or not we can take citizenship from a person. What is important here is that the European Court of Justice demands that the withdrawal be so-called proportional. Proportionality is an expression that the European Court of Justice is pleased to use, because it leaves the impression that the Member States are left with real leeway and that this room for manoeuvre is based on fairness. However, it is merely a question of Denmark's right to pursue an independent immigration policy being significantly restricted, and we do not know to what extent, because the European Court of Justice will not tell us. The uncertainty as to when something is proportional means, in practice, that the elected representatives do not know what Denmark can or cannot do, and the European Court of Justice will further reduce this unclear scope by a later judgment. Secondly, there is another important recent judgment, Case C-561/14, which has demanded that Denmark amend its legislation so that Turkish citizens in a number of intrusive areas should not be treated differently from EU citizens. The result is that we cannot pursue an independent foreign policy towards Turkey. And what is the background to this case? The basis is the Ankara Agreement, which is the oldest association agreement between the EU, the then EC, and a third country. Suddenly, the European Court of Justice has prevented us from pursuing an independent foreign policy towards Turkish citizens if they are economically active. In this case too, it is quite unclear whether Denmark has left a real room for manoeuvre to pursue an independent foreign policy towards Turkish citizens as soon as they are economically active. We want to know what these cases mean for Denmark's right to pursue an independent foreign policy, and we want to know what degree of detail there is to relate to a degree of detail that the voters can also relate to. We therefore support DF's proposal to clarify the EU's impact on Danish immigration policy. The European Court of Justice influences our legislation and forces us to correct and change legal practices and practices despite the Danish legal requirement. The Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration evidently believes that an investigation does not measure up to the result that can be expected to come out of the work. The Minister justifies his rejection of an explanation that such work would require time and resources for the Ministry. The answer given by the Minister must mean that the government does not consider a defence of Danish foreign law to be particularly important. New citizens therefore support the proposers' proposal to clarify the EU's impact on Danish immigration policy. If I have to sum up some of what has been said here, I think it might be quite nice to say to people that if they want to have an overview, they can just create it themselves. In other words, if the ministry and the minister think it is too big a task, it is probably also a pretty big task if individuals are to do so, whether politicians or citizens, if they are to go in and find out all the different laws and other things that have been adopted over time, in order to get an overview. Then I think we should also remember that this is not a random area. Foreign policy is one of the most important areas that the population considers to be. And now my speaking time is up. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212134917	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212134940	With this motion for a resolution, the Danish People's Party proposes to set a ceiling on the number of people who are granted Danish citizenship each year by amending the agreement on citizenship. More specifically, the Danish People's Party proposes that a maximum of 1,000 persons per year be granted Danish citizenship by naturalisation or by declaration. The proposal has been tabled quite a few times before. In fact, we have been in the archives and found that Mrs Pia Kjærsgaard, on behalf of the Progress Party, made a similar proposal back in 1992 which was rejected by the then Socialist Minister, Mrs Pia Gjellerup. Later it has been presented in 1994 and 1995 and 1996 and also back in 2017 and in the negotiations of a new birthright circular in 2018. This time too, a social democratic government cannot support the proposal. After all, the rule of the Constitution is that Danish citizenship can only be granted by law. It is therefore the Folketing which specifically decides who is to be a Danish national, and in practice this is done according to certain fixed criteria for Danish citizenship, which are laid down politically in a political agreement. On the basis of these criteria, applications for Danish citizenship are dealt with and, fortunately, there is a broad majority in the Folketing, including the rapporteur for the proposers' own party, who is behind these criteria. The Government does not believe that the right of birth should be based on a first-come, first-served principle and thus depend on whether or not the ceiling was applied for before the ceiling was reached. The granting of Danish citizenship should, in the view of the Government, be based on the requirements and objective criteria agreed by the parties to the agreement with the nationality agreement of 29 June 2018. This is done on the basis of an approach to conduct a tight but not a crazy alien policy. That is clear, among other things. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the motion for a resolution states that it is something very special and a gift to have Danish citizenship, and it is therefore essential, according to the proposal, to make sure that the foreigners who receive citizenship are really Danish. In addition, the proposal states that the selection of the 1.000 persons must be based on criteria relating, inter alia, to: language testing and passing a citizenship test. In addition, people from the North and South Schleswig must take precedence over other applicants. I agree with the authors of the motion that it is something special to become a Danish citizen, and it is precisely on that basis that we make high but objective and reasonable demands on those who want to be Danish citizens. To become a Danish citizen should not be something that comes to sleep, and I actually do not think it is today either. Applicants must, for example: document that they can speak, that they are self-supporting, that they stay out of crime and that they have a knowledge of Danish social conditions. I believe, basically, that if you meet the strict requirements, you should also be able to become a Danish citizen. It is simply not fair that an applicant who has had his life here in Denmark and who meets all the requirements to become a Danish national is met with a refusal simply because he makes his application later than others. Imagine that the American citizen, who most of his adult life has lived in Denmark, has become Danish married, has had children, has grown up in Denmark, or has had children who have grown up in Denmark, and have worked in Denmark, must have a refusal because she happens to be no. 1.001. I don't think that would be fair. Several of the demands made in the motion for a resolution actually apply today. This applies, among other things, to: the requirement for the applicants' Danish knowledge and for the applicant to pass the citizenship test. In addition, there is already less access to Danish citizenship for Nordic citizens and Danish-minded Southern Schleswigers. As you know, the birthright agreement was concluded in the summer of 2018, when the conditions for obtaining Danish citizenship were established. I am very pleased with that agreement and I am also very pleased with the political cooperation surrounding that agreement, so I cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution. Thank you for the floor.

On this point, although it may come as a surprise to some people, the Radical Left has also moved, and today we think it is logical that we should of course meet some requirements in order to obtain citizenship and not just get it in order to be integrated afterwards. You have to be integrated before you get it. That is why there are some of the demands contained in the current legislation and the current set of rules that we actually support, for example. that one should know Danish, that one should know something about Denmark, that one should have been in employment, that one should have lived here for a certain period, and that one should not have committed serious crime. There are also some things that we do not support. We think it must be the case that completing primary school must be able to do so in the face of a test of citizenship. I find it a little difficult to understand what the majority here in the Danish Parliament thinks is wrong with primary school, since one obviously does not learn enough about Denmark and therefore has to get up to some test in addition to having completed primary school. We also think that people born and raised in Denmark should have automatic citizenship when they are 18 unless they have committed, for example. serious crime. We also think it must be the case that if you have a good reason not to have been in work, learned Danish or passed the citizenship test, for example. Because you are disabled, you must automatically be able to obtain dispensation if you have a good reason and can prove it. When we look at the demands that are, and which we think are reasonable some way along the way, we also think that it must be the case that those who then meet those requirements can have citizenship, whether it is 2 000 people or 200 people. That is why, of course, we do not agree with the Danish People's Party's proposal that a ceiling of 1,000 people per year should be set that can become citizens. If there are 1 200 people who meet these requirements, which are fairly strict, but also to some extent fair and reasonable, we think that they should all have citizenship. We also think it is worth bearing in mind that, from our point of view, it is very welcome if people want to be citizens and thus become citizens of the country in which they now live and choose to become Danish, also on paper. I think that basically is good, and it is also basically good that we then make some demands on what to live up to in order to be called Dane. In those words, we do not support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212154445	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212154505	Thank you for the floor. The motion for a resolution states that a given nationality must be able to be withdrawn again for a period of 10 years after the award if, during the trial period, the person concerned commits serious crime which triggers a six-month prison sentence or more. It does not matter whether the penalty is conditional or unconditional. Let me say straight away that the government can easily follow the ideas behind the resolution. I myself find it frustrating to read in the media about people who have acquired Danish citizenship and who subsequently commit serious crime. Having said that, the government cannot support the resolution. A stronger claim is already being made to applicants for Danish citizenship and a disqualification or withdrawal of a person's nationality may take place if he or she has been guilty of fraud in connection with the acquisition of his or her nationality, provided that he or she has committed terrorist crimes or has acted in a manner which seriously harms the vital interests of the country as it is called. The government is of the opinion that one should draw a line in the sand in relation to the applicants and say: In connection with the Danish Parliament's examination of a bill on citizenship notification, you must meet the high requirements for obtaining Danish citizenship, and if you do so, you can become Danish. I would like to explain that a little further. The Social Democratic Party, together with the previous government and the rapporteur for the party of the proposers in the summer of 2018, tightened the standard of good conduct so that several types of serious crime can result in an applicant being excluded from becoming a Danish citizen. With the birthright agreement of the summer of 2018, persons who have received an unconditional sentence of liberty of one year or more are permanently excluded from Danish citizenship, just as persons who have been sentenced to an unconditional sentence of 3 months or more for personal crimes, violence against children and sexual crimes, as a starting point, will be excluded. The requirement that an applicant should not have committed crime must be fulfilled right up to the time of the person's acquisition of nationality, and for persons who are subject to the requirement to participate in a constitutional ceremony, the new rules will therefore say quite the way to the holding of the constitutional ceremony. Today, therefore, there is already a fairly tighter requirement for applicants for Danish citizenship. In addition, it will be possible, under the circumstances, to disclaim the Danish nationality by judgment if it has been obtained by fraudulent acts committed prior to the granting of the nationality. It is not a requirement for the person to have dual citizenship in such a situation. It was also agreed with the Birthright Agreement of 2018 that two years after the adoption of a law on citizenship, a systematic assessment should be made of whether there are grounds for initiating involvement proceedings due to the concealment of criminal acts committed prior to the application for Danish citizenship. In addition, it was agreed that, as a one-off measure for persons who have been informed of Danish citizenship since 2014, it will be assessed whether there is a basis for initiating an withdrawal proceedings due to the concealment of criminal acts committed prior to the application for Danish citizenship. Against this background earlier this year, the Ministry initiated a review of cases relating to the persons who have acquired Danish citizenship since 2014 as part of the follow-up to the agreement on citizenship, and until now the Ministry has found approx. 80 cases where discrepancies have been found between what the applicant has provided in connection with his application for Danish citizenship and the information now available in the Criminal Register. The Ministry has currently sent 18 cases to the police requesting that the police investigate whether the person concerned has intentionally supplied false or misleading information or concealed relevant information in connection with the application for citizenship. I will, of course, inform the entire Parliamentary Committee of the final results of the examination as soon as we have examined the cases. If, after obtaining Danish citizenship, a person is convicted of a violation of what is called Chapters 12 and 13 of the Penal Code, they act among other things. on terrorism and other crimes against the security of the State... there will be the possibility of disclaiming citizenship by judgment, unless he becomes a stateless person. In addition, as a recent addition, it has become possible to take administratively away Danish citizenship from persons who have taken a course of action which seriously harms the vital interests of the country, unless they become stateless. So far, three people have been deprived of Danish citizenship administratively over the last few weeks. One of the international conventions in the field of citizenship, signed and ratified by Denmark, is the Convention on the Rights of Nationals and contains, inter alia: the possibility of withdrawing citizenship, and this is where the wording for the person to be seriously detrimental to the vital interests of the Contracting State comes from; that is if a person has dual nationality. However, the motion for a resolution we are debating today goes further than that, since, according to the proposal, ordinary crime must also lead to a withdrawal. As I have explained today, we have created an extended room for manoeuvre in order to exclude criminal applicants or to withdraw citizenship. If you grant a person Danish citizenship and then allow citizenship to be conditional on the person concerned not committing serious crime for a period of 10 years, which triggers a prison sentence of 6 months in prison or more, it will mean that a person is effectively deprived of Danish citizenship because of ordinary crime, and that would be contrary to this convention of national law. As I said, you should at some point draw a line in the sand. As far as I am concerned, it makes very good sense to draw that line as a starting point when it comes to the granting of citizenship. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212163502	The debate is open, and that is the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212163525	The motion for a resolution is about the government being instructed by the end of this Assembly to present different models of how screening talks can be held with either all or selected applicants for citizenship. I would like to state straight away that I very much agree that it is deeply frustrating when people who have acquired Danish citizenship later take actions that show that they are against everything that democracy is about. Today it is already the case that applicants for Danish citizenship must meet the same strict requirement not to have committed crime in order to become Danish citizens. In addition, a person can today be deprived of his Danish citizenship in certain situations. A person applying for Danish citizenship must state by faith and law whether he or she has committed crime in this country or abroad. To this end, the applicant must pledge allegiance and loyalty to Denmark and the Danish society and declare that he wishes to comply with Danish law and respect fundamental Danish legal principles as well as to comply with the constitution and respect fundamental Danish values. In addition, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration shall check what is known as the Criminal Register to see whether there is information that the person is charged or convicted for matters which exclude the person from becoming a Danish national either temporarily or permanently. This is done not only once, but several times during the handling of a case. In addition, the Police Intelligence Service assesses whether a person who stands to become a Danish citizen may be a threat to the security of the state. Finally, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration considers, as something new 2 years after the adoption of a law on citizenship notification, whether there is a basis for initiating a case of disqualification on account of the concealment of criminal acts committed prior to the notification of Danish citizenship. All these steps aim to ensure that people who are granted Danish citizenship meet the demands and expectations of new members of Danish society. In the context of the new birthright agreement of June 2018, it was introduced that applicants should in future participate in a constitutional ceremony in order to become Danish citizens. At the ceremony, applicants must show respect for Danish values and respect for representatives of the authorities and sign to comply with the Constitution and respect fundamental Danish values and legal principles, including Danish democracy. The case of an applicant, inter alia: Signing on to comply with Danish legislation, and where it subsequently turns out that at the time of signature the applicant did not intend to comply with Danish legislation, it will be possible, from the outset, to be subject to the rules of the birthright law on disqualification due to fraud. If an applicant has broken the law and has failed to disclose or supplied false or misleading information on the application, he or she will be entitled, after the acquisition, to refuse the right of nationality by judgment on the grounds of fraud if the fraudulent relationship has led to the acquisition. In addition, the person convicted of violation of one or more of the provisions of Chapters 12 and 13 of the Criminal Code on, inter alia, terror, by judgment can be denied its Danish citizenship, unless the person becomes stateless. Finally, with the latest amendment to the law of citizenship, access to administrative withdrawal of citizenship has been established if a person has acted in a manner which seriously harms the vital interests of the country. As a follow-up to the 2018 citizenship agreement, a consultation was launched with a large number of countries inside and outside the EU, where their experience with screening of applicants before granting citizenship was questioned. The hearings were sent to the Parliamentary Committee on the Rights of the Birth in September of this year. That is clear, among other things. of the consultation answers that certain countries use application forms and interviews with applicants to screen applicants for their anti-democratic positions. However, it is not the case that we can simply copy another country's system. In many other countries, the granting of citizenship is a task for administration and administration. In Denmark, citizenship is acquired by law. This has to be taken into account when considering how to establish a system of screening in Denmark. The establishment of a possible screening scheme in Denmark should be seen in the light of and play together with the checks of applicants already carried out today before the award, as well as the possibilities for subsequent withdrawal or withdrawal of Danish citizenship. In addition, a possible screening scheme raises a great deal of administrative and legal challenges that would need to be clarified, as well as some important political issues that need to be addressed. This concerns, among other things: information to be obtained, how they are provided and how they are weighted. What, then, are the positions that the applicant must not have? Do you have to google yourself? Should you see what people write on Facebook or Instagram? And are we really going to find out what we want? If we ask people whether they are against democracy or Danish values, it is not certain that we will get a fair answer. In addition, the collection of information on applicants will have to be carried out in compliance with international obligations and applicants must not be discriminated against on the basis, for example, of: their ethnicity, religion or political beliefs. This means that any screening scheme would have to meet objective and objective criteria. A system of selection of candidates to be interviewed will also have to be based on objective and objective criteria. A screening scheme also raises the question of who is to carry out the screening, including taking part in any conversations. If it is imagined that the Committee of Insights will have to have a conversation with all or with selected applicants, it can considerably increase the committee's work and extend the time spent on the proceedings of the applicants. As I said, there are quite a few questions. I've raised some of these. I would also like to help answer the questions, because I really think that the proposers have something important to do with them. However, if a screening system is to work in practice and not just look good on paper for all of us, I believe that we need to invest some time and effort at a political level in thinking about and discussing what we want to do with the scheme and, on that basis, see how it should be put together. This is a task which the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration could well start tomorrow and, as proposed, send a note on this before the end of this parliamentary assembly, but I would really rather that these discussions take place a little more politically. It could be in the Committee on Bodily Rights or in the circle behind the agreement on citizenship. A possible screening scheme raises a great deal of administrative and legal challenges which the Ministry would, of course, very much like to help uncover, but it also raises, in my view, some important political issues which we also think should be addressed along the way. That is why I think it is important for us politically to get involved in the process as early as possible. It could, for example, be done. be by visiting together one or more of the countries that have experience in conducting interviews with applicants. There could also be other concrete steps that we could take. With those words, I would like to thank you and I look forward to following the committee's examination of this issue.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212172404	Thank you. In our view, we have given citizenship to too many people over the last 40 years and also to the wrong people. We therefore believe that the current rules alone are not enough to look at easily verifiable requirements, for example how long one has lived in Denmark, whether one speaks roughly Danish, and whether one is convicted of severe crime. We must insist that it is a great privilege to acquire Danish citizenship, and that is why much more needs to be done than just a technical assessment, as is the case today. We therefore believe that a screening interview should also be introduced as part of the process to obtain Danish citizenship, a conversation in which the applicant's background and attitudes can be further questioned. We know that a conversation will not catch anyone who wishes Denmark ill • we also mentioned here in the debate before that one can of course lie that if you are a good actor you can cheat yourself through a conversation; we are fully aware of that but it is not an argument against having a conversation that it will not catch everyone. The question is whether it will catch some, and we think it will. There are other countries where similar procedures are already in place when granting citizenship, and we could benefit from that experience. We also have systems in Denmark that are already somewhat similar to what we imagine, such as the session in the defence, and we also have our system of judges, where mainly in the political parties the judges are appointed, who also make some very important decisions in the courts. It can be done in many different ways, and we are open to several different models. We are also open to listening to experiences from abroad. We think that is very sensible. In the motion for a resolution, we only ask the government to examine different models of how to carry out a screening interview. We believe that in the Conservative People's Party. Citizenship is not something you are entitled to. This is something we can give to people if we want to let them into Denmark and let them have that ownership for our country, and if we do not want it, we can not give it to them. That is why the Constitution is crystal clear: that it is the Folketing that gives citizenship that it does not take place in a court, that it does not take place on the basis of equal treatment principles, but that it takes place on the basis of what the Danish people want through their elected representatives. It is therefore very important to maintain that it is the Folketing that decides and that we should only give citizenship to people that we can use in Denmark. There are two different ways of looking at the granting of citizenship. There I have also noticed that there are people in particular in the Committee on the Rights of the Child who think that citizenship should be given to people for whom we think it is a pity. Then there is a different approach, and that is the approach that I and the Conservative People's Party have and that is that we should give citizenship to people that we can use for something in Denmark. That is why we do not sit and give citizenship to people who cannot pass a Danish test because they, for example, are not able to pass a Danish test. have five different psychic diagnoses, because we have no benefit from sheep ownership, and they cannot meaningfully cast a vote for a parliamentary election. There I just think that what is the dividing line in this debate is whether we give citizenship to people, because it is a pity for them, or we give it to them, because we, who represent the Danish people, think that it is for the benefit of Denmark that they get it.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212173052	Thank you. This proposal is, after all, a fine and logical extension of the previous motions for resolutions. It appears from the proposal that the proposer admits that too many foreigners who should not have had citizenship have been granted citizenship, and that is very good with self-insight, so much praise for it. I would also like to agree with the author of the motion that citizenship is something special. As I have also explained in the two previous motions for resolutions, we in the New Citizen regard citizenship as the most important gift, and that is why we must, of course, safeguard that gift. We must be much more restrictive with the privilege of becoming a Danish citizen. For decades, citizenship has been generously distributed to too many people who do not share our values at all or will contribute to Danish society in a positive way, and we must stop doing so. The proposal we are considering here is that the government should examine different models of how to conduct a screening interview with people who want Danish citizenship. That's a really nice intention. A personal interview will be a fine tool to get a sense of who we are facing: Is it an applicant who really wants Denmark and the Danish community, or is it an applicant who will never contribute to the Danish community, either in spirit or deed? New citizens support the proposal and will make a constructive contribution in committee to clarifying how models can be combined. The starting point is that it must be members of the Folketing who hold the talks. We cannot delegate a subjective assessment of the suitability of persons to the official apparatus. It can be used for an objective assessment of criteria, but not for the subjective experience of applicants. It is the members of the Folketing who grant citizenship by law, and therefore it must also be the members of the Folketing who are responsible for the talks. It then also sets a perfectly natural limit on how many applicants can be called for interviews within a year. The comments on the proposal show that the proposer opens the way for PET to inform the Committee on the Rights of Citizens whether there are people on the bill for citizenship who have anti-democratic views, and this illustrates very well how blind citizenship has been distributed. How can one seriously claim that people with anti-democratic attitudes should even come close to being admitted to the Danish community and equipped with Danish citizenship? How have we got so far that we are not just using our basic common sense to say that, of course, foreigners who display anti-democratic attitudes should not get anywhere near becoming Danish citizens at all? For it is glaringly naive, but nevertheless it has been so for too many years. We must stop this, and any action in this direction is welcome, and that is why we also welcome this action, and we look forward to the committee reading and support the proposal.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212173407	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Henrik Dahl, Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212173414	Thank you. As I also mentioned in my question before to Mr. Peder Hvelplund, it is not a freedom to become a Danish citizen. It is not a social right, nor is it a human right. If you are to decide to be a Danish citizen at all conceptually, you have to say that Danish citizenship is a privilege. It must be understood relatively literally; a privilege is a collective right which is granted to a particular group of people. A privilege is a good word, because it is actually the best way to determine citizenship. It is a part of the collective right to govern Denmark, which is at the heart of citizenship. What is also crucial about the Constitution is that it is a political decision. It is not a legal decision or an administrative decision. The Constitution clearly states that it is a political decision Therefore, of course, we politicians need help to make the best decisions we can. We should not give shares in the collective right to govern Denmark to people who intend to destroy the country. That is certainly my political position. But the question is how to avoid making that mistake. For the past has shown that it can be difficult to assign citizenship to the right people and to avoid the wrong people. Therefore, it is a good idea to study more closely how the Folketing can strengthen its decision-making power and decision-making power, namely how we can better exercise our right to give foreigners a share in the collective right to control Denmark. That is why LA supports the proposal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212174932	Thank you to the rapporteur for the explanatory statement. We shall now proceed to the answer to the question, which is the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212174941	Thank you, President. I would like to start by thanking the Left, who asked me to explain how the government will prevent higher public services for refugees and immigrants from leading to increased influx of asylum into Denmark. We in the government want to maintain the strict immigration policy that a broad majority in the Folketing is behind, because we want the influx to be controlled and because we want foreigners who come here to contribute as much as possible to Danish society. And we agree that we ensure this first and foremost by getting people into work. I fully agree that the level of benefits, together with a number of other at least equally important factors, can help to make it more or less attractive to come to Denmark. I have clearly said this on 13 December 2018, and I would very much like to say it again today. I am also quite sure that I have said this before 13 December 2018, because I believe that there is an obvious link there. That is also why we, as a government, do not want new arrivals of refugees to have full cash assistance from day one. But that must never be the only consideration when we organise public services. If that were the only consideration and one had to follow such a logic completely, we would have to dismantle the welfare state altogether. After all, I don't think any of us want that. There is a balance to be found, and that is also why the government has proposed a temporary grant for a number of families with little money. It's a bill that the Danish Parliament is dealing with these days. It must provide financial support for the support of children whose parents are in the cash assistance system and who are reduced by either the ceiling on cash benefits or because they receive integration benefits. This is a temporary solution until, hopefully, on the basis of recommendations from a performance committee, by a broad majority in the Folketing, we can negotiate a new and more balanced system of benefits, just as we have said countless times before the parliamentary elections. We are setting up this committee for that purpose, because we need a system of benefits where there is still a principle of income for new arrivals, so that they only have to contribute for a period of time before they can benefit fully from Danish transfer income. Our position is that a new system should support children growing up under reasonable conditions, that it should pay off to work and be self-supporting, and finally that the level of benefits does not in itself become an independent attraction in the asylum system. In addition, it is also important not only to focus narrowly on the level of performance itself. We also need to discuss the link between rights and obligations which, in our view, are not properly balanced in existing legislation. The social contract, which is based precisely on rights and obligations, is not reflected in the current integration effort, and we will therefore introduce a duty for newcomers and immigrants with an integration need in the cash aid system to deliver an effort corresponding to a full 37 hours working week, both as much as possible making a contribution to Danish society when they are here, but also because we know that it is absolutely crucial for the individual's integration into Danish society that one finds that there is a demand to deliver an effort. It's one purpose. Another aim is to counteract the increased influx of asylum to Denmark. It is just as important if not even more important to the government. Let me repeat here what I have already said on several occasions. Paragraph 1: It is an ambition of the government that we ourselves can control how many foreigners come to Denmark. That is why we are pursuing a policy of reducing the spontaneous number of asylum seekers and, in return, we have agreed to participate in the United Nations quota refugee programme. Paragraph 2: We must use the surplus now that we have low asylum rates to increase our focus on helping more and better in the neighbourhoods of refugees. Paragraph 3: We must use the current surplus to strengthen the integration of foreigners who have already come here. In fact, I find that policy widely supported by the people. It is a policy that has meant that the asylum influx to Denmark is now under control. I expect record low asylum figures for Denmark here in 2019. It is also a policy in which there is such broad support in this Parliament that we can say that the main pillars of foreign policy are today firmly established. It's historic, and it's good. After all, for the last five decades of significant immigration into this country has created major problems, even greater than most had foreseen. I am thinking here that we have a joint responsibility in this Chamber. I was born in 1981. About two out of three years ago there have been blue governments; there have also been long periods of red governments, and none of us can seem to be completely discredited for how immigration policy has developed during that period. Of course, you can mourn that if you want to, but we cannot turn back time and change the past. On the other hand, we can do what we can to change the future, because we are not at all done with the work in the field of aliens and integration. But I also think it is important to stress from the government's point of view that there is no reason to panic either. Things take time before they work, and we are not going to finish the work just for the time being, and we must, of course, be persistent. I have therefore chosen to see the glass half full, and I also think that I am constantly being confirmed in it, both because the asylum figures are low, because new arrivals are getting significantly better in jobs, because more people are being trained and because the value studies that are being carried out, although we are still far from achieving their goal, also show that, after all, the vast majority of immigrants support ordinary democratic values. It is also essential to stick to it. I believe that this is the result of the fact that we are gradually approaching a situation where we can say that the broad policy on foreigners is really starting to work. That is why it is also essential that we continue to pursue it: a strict immigration policy that maintains a low influx and supports the work and support of refugees and immigrants and the values on which our society is based, democratic values. That is how I believe we can preserve and develop the society we all love, even if the government has set up a Services Commission to find better balance in the existing system of benefits and to ensure a better balance between the right and duty of the cash benefits system. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20191212191509	The question has been called for here in order to basically highlight that the government is in the process of relaxing foreign policy. But there is no doubt that the government has embarked on a process of relaxing foreign policy in a wide range of areas. And it is almost absurd to hear one Socialist after another say that it is not a relaxation of foreign policy that we welcome more refugees to Denmark and that it is not a relaxation of foreign policy that we give them more money when they come to Denmark. I think people can see that it is, of course. But the Socialists have obviously decided that this concession will simply not be granted because they know that the next question will be: how can it be that the policy of foreigners is relaxed when Mette Frederiksen, during the election campaign, solemnly promised that she would not do so? I am not going to form a government that relaxes foreign policy. I would like to make that clear again. Then, of course, it is problematic to admit that you are actually relaxing foreign policy, even though it is quite obvious that this is what you are doing. Foreign policy is a lot of things, but it is basically about the conditions that you give to people who come to Denmark. It is, of course, directly about which criteria are used to allow you to stay in Denmark. It is also about the granting of citizenship, namely how we treat foreigners in terms of obtaining permanent residence permits and citizenship. And, of course, it is also about what conditions we give foreigners in Denmark. And I also believe that both the Socialists and the Left will recognise that such things as where you live and which centre you are going to live on are, of course, foreign policy. And in the same way it is also foreign policy how much money you get when you come to Denmark. So immigration policy is all the conditions that we provide for foreigners to come here and stay in Denmark. We have pursued a strict foreign policy in the years in which we sat in government. And that is basically a whole series of decisions that are not really very pleasant to make. Well, it's not very pleasant to have to tell a lot of people that they can't live in Denmark and that they can't stay here, even if they could have a much better life in Denmark than elsewhere. It is not very liberal, nor is it very nice to have to do so. It is also not very nice to have to tell people that the benefits they can get when they come to Denmark must be relatively low. But if we do not pursue strict foreign policy, we look into a scenario for Denmark's future, which is very, very unpleasant. We are going to have very big social clashes; we are going to have social poverty; we are going to have an underclass which is very much going to be made up of people with a foreign background, and thus a very divided society. It is very, very serious and it is probably the greatest challenge for our generation of politicians to avoid that we end up there. For then we will destroy the trusting and free society that we have in Denmark, what we all love. And there you have to have the big picture in mind. One cannot strike down one small element of foreign policy and say that this is not so nice, so we will not. All in all, we need to defend that the immigration policy we are pursuing needs to be strict in order to avoid the scenario of the future. We tightened up a lot, and that meant that Denmark went from a 5th place in the EU, compared to how many asylum seekers came to Denmark when the bourgeois government came to in 2015, to that we went down on, I think it was a 17th place when we resigned. According to the Department of Integration's calculations, this meant that approx. 50,000 fewer asylum seekers came to Denmark. It was not because asylum influx to Europe was generally lower, but, of course, it also contributed to much fewer. But the move from lying in a 5th place down to, as I remember, a 17th place meant 50,000 fewer asylum seekers for Denmark. And if you roll back the immigration policy, it will have the opposite effect; then there will be more, and we are talking about figures of that magnitude. That is why it is absolutely essential that the government stays on track and that the government disregards its support parties and conducts the strict immigration policy available to the bourgeois parties to continue. And it is entirely up to the Socialists whether they want to do it or not, because they have the opportunity to come over to us and not to pursue foreign policy with the left.

Then the exception is that you can get sick or you can get unemployed, and fortunately we have a welfare society that holds hands under you. But at the moment that you are granted a residence permit in Adelgade in Copenhagen by the Refugee Board, what is normal? Then the normal in Denmark is actually that you start your life in Denmark on a public transfer. It's a hole in the head. We meet people with the reverse logic of what we think should be the guiding principle of Danish society. That is why we on the part of the government are now saying'and we said on the part of the Social Democratic Party before the elections: let us try to start by meeting people with a new logic. Let's start by saying to people, "Go find a job." If you can't find a job, you're welcome to show up at the municipality, and then we have a 37-hour integration program that you can participate in, which consists of Danish teaching, activation and other things, and to the extent that you show up, you get a benefit to the extent that you don't show up, you don't get a benefit. And if you're 2 hours late, you don't get yelled at and get sanctioned and get called for interview at the job center, you just don't get any benefits. In fact, I think that most newly arrived refugees would think that was a perfectly common sound principle and, by the way, a very generous principle in relation to the principles of the social systems in the countries from which they come. And in the opinion of the Radical Left, it is because it has not dared to reform the general energy taxes in this House. District heating companies are obliged to take the cheapest offer. It is written in the Act on Heat Supply that you have to take the cheapest offer. This is because district heating plants have a monopoly: only one pipe goes out to each house, to each radiator, and therefore the regulation has been made to ensure that consumers get the cheapest possible. Then you have one fuel, namely chips, wood on which no tax is levied, and it goes without saying that all other projects will then have to compete with a duty-free fuel. The same applies to excess heat, as does heating from electricity, from wind turbines and other renewable energy sources. And it is not ideal either for the climate or for the economy to sit and choose some bad projects for society, for the climate, but for them to be chosen because, in isolation, they are cheapest because there is no tax on them. Therefore, the competitiveness of surplus heat compared to other projects will never be good until an overall reform of energy taxes is made, so that what is cheapest and best for society and also cheapest and best for the climate is promoted. So this is a little help, but not the full solution. And we would certainly like to be involved in discussing further initiatives if there is any courage to do so. ParlaMint-DK_20191212202354	Thank you. Well, then it's the tax minister. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20191212202404	Thank you for that, President. I would like to start by thanking you for receiving the bill, which implements parts of the agreement on increased use of surplus heat, which was concluded between all parties of the Danish Parliament back in March 2019. One of the key elements of the proposal is the abolition of the current remuneration tax, which means that surplus heat is taxed at a percentage of the price paid by the heating companies for the heat. This has proved to be an inappropriate way of taxing surplus heat, as it is not clearly defined which costs can and must be included in the remuneration. This gives rise to uncertainty and also a certain coincidence in taxation. After all, as we know, many surplus heat projects require large investments and long-term budgeting, and uncertainty about the framework conditions hampers the enterprise's desire to make the necessary investments. As a consequence, it is proposed that the remuneration tax should be abolished and replaced by a uniform tax of DKK 25. per gigajoule heat. For companies that are part of an agreement arrangement with the Danish Energy Agency, a reduced rate of DKK 10 is proposed, as I said. per gigajoule heat. The new flat-rate rates will remove the uncertainty I mentioned earlier about the framework conditions, which the current rules have thus led to, and will be considerably easier to administer, both for companies and, above all, for tax administration. Then there is also a transitional arrangement. Although the new rules are generally considered to be better than those in force, it cannot be ruled out that there are currently agreements with companies, i.e. here heating companies, where a very low price has been agreed for surplus heat and where district heating customers therefore have low heating costs. The draft law therefore provides for transitional arrangements for existing suppliers of surplus heat, which will be able to remain in the current rules for a period of up to 15 years. As you know, the overall reorganisation of the rules, as has also been discussed, must be approved by the European Commission before it can be put into effect, so that companies can be sure that the future framework conditions are also compatible with the EU's State aid rules. Thank you for the debate. It is a broad agreement that has been made, and I am, of course, available to answer the questions raised in this Chamber about the agreement that a broad majority of the Folketing has made.

Mr. Hans Christian Schmidt and Mr. Ole Birk Olesen, because we have managed to get to where we are now, namely that there has been a road package.
