At some point, of course, this must be implemented not only in Danish legislation but also in the legislation of all other European countries. We will be able to control vehicles much better with advanced tachographs, which tell us not only whether you comply with your driving and rest periods but also where you are. In other words, it will also be possible to check whether the vehicle in which drivers drive has also had the statutory journey back to the country of origin of the vehicle, which is among other things. is part of the road package, i.e. that the vehicle must return at least every 56 days to the country of origin where it comes from. In many ways, the road package will change the whole way in which we work on road transport and, above all, give us quite different opportunities to be able to enforce the rules by authority. So it's really positive. I would also like to make it clear that I also feel able to thank my predecessor, the previous government, for the clear inspiration given to the legislative proposal we have here, since it was the case that on 2 May this year, a little over 7 months ago, the then government played out with the package called 'Safeness, fairness and orderly relations' presented by the then Prime Minister. And this publication mentions the initiative we are working on here, in the clear indication that there should be a significantly increased level of fines for the illegal holding of rest in vehicles, and that is precisely what we are implementing here with the proposed legislative proposal. That is why, of course, I am somewhat surprised at some of the statements that have been made during the debate by the previous government parties, which now believe that this significantly higher level of fines should not quite be as significant as it was apparently thought when you were in government. Nevertheless, there has been a desire to table amendments, and of course we will always have to deal with this, as we are doing now, and I can only call on the rapporteurs to ask questions about the elements that need to be clarified. I also think, for the sake of good order, that it should be said that driving-rest-time rules have two considerations. One is indeed to ensure road safety, and the other is social considerations. And especially the part about having a regular weekly rest of 45 hours is, of course, about ensuring that an employee can also have reasonable relationships with his family. And it is the case that this long weekly rest must not be held in the vehicle. Needless to say, it also requires planning. During the debate it has been argued that this level of fines would prevent Danish drivers from starting in the industry. And I must admit that I may find a rather strange argument, since it is precisely about the long regular weekly rest. And one must assume that a Danish driver driving in Denmark has a reasonable opportunity to come home and spend the weekend. It may well be that the weekend doesn't fall Saturday and Sunday, but at another time, but at least you will normally also have the long rest period you have to have within a week, at home. And immediately we don't know about examples of "I'm not going to be able to deny that there can be examples" that it's Danish drivers who violate this particular rule. On the other hand, it is a typical problem in relation to foreign drivers, which is why the previous government also took the initiative, among other things. to increase the effort in relation to issuing parking tickets at rest places, so that you could prevent it is in relation to this 25-hour rule that you just held the long rests on state rest places. Because drivers are not supposed to have them there. It is not just a question of the forthcoming road package; it is, in fact, already in force in the EU law, as several have also highlighted. The long weekly rest must be kept in reasonable and orderly conditions. You have to have access to proper facilities, you have to be able to bathe, you have to be able to relax, you have to be able to lie in a proper bed and not in the car. That is why, as I said, this proposal, which provides a framework for the level of fines: for the company DKK 20,000. and to the driver 10,000 kroner. And in relation to this, I think we should remember that it has also been mentioned from several quarters whether there are others that can be held responsible. I know that this is a debate that Mr. Henning Hyllested has also had several laps, and Mr. Hans Kristian Skibby also mentioned it. Compared to that, it is worth bearing in mind that in the Running-Rise-Time Regulation, there is in fact a provision which lays down an obligation for freight forwarders to ensure that contractual transport timetables are in accordance with the rules on Running-Rise-Time. And if it is the case that the freight forwarder's timetable for transport is not in line with the rules on driving-rest time, the freight forwarder can actually be fined and imprisoned for up to 4 months. We would, of course, like to elaborate on this in committee so that they can be defined in greater detail. In other words, freight forwarders can be penalised for complicity in breaching the rules on driving rest time under the general rules of the criminal code on participation liability, and freight forwarders will therefore be liable, as appropriate, for the breach of the rules on driving rest time by the carrier and/or the driver. However, it should be stressed that, where appropriate, it is the police and the prosecutor who decide whether there is a basis for prosecuting a freight forwarder for complicity in the infringement of the transport undertaking and/or the driver. And, of course, this also presupposes that the burden of proof can be lifted. However, I think that should be mentioned in this debate. Otherwise, I would like to thank you for the positive commitments that have been made during the debate. We will, of course, address any questions that may be raised in committee and I look forward to further work.
